Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. It’s a lovely office you have here, very bright. Well, as to why I'm here... I've been thinking about coming in for a while now, and my daughter actually finally pushed me to make the call. You see, for the past year or so, I just feel like I’m always... well, always puttering around. I'm constantly fixing things around the house, arranging the cupboards, reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month. And it's not just at home, I keep volunteering for just one more thing down at the church or the library committee. 

It’s gotten to the point where, even at 87 years old—when most people my age are perfectly happy resting in a nice armchair—I just have such a terrible time sitting down and being still. I try to sit and watch a program on PBS or read a book, but five minutes in, I’m up again because I remembered a picture frame that needs dusting or a hinge that needs oiling. It's just constant activity, from the moment I wake up until I finally exhaust myself at night, and... well, I just don't know why I can't seem to just stop and relax like a normal person my age. I should be enjoying my retirement, but there's always a project, always something to fuss with, and I just can't seem to turn it off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. It’s not worry at all. My daughter actually asked me that exact same thing—she said, "Mom, are you anxious about something?" But I’m really not! My friend Martha, now she’s a worrier. She paces the floor because she’s fretting over her grandson’s grades or whether her pension check is going to be late, and she gets all tied up in knots. But for me, my mind is perfectly fine. I don't have any of those nervous, fearful thoughts about bad things happening. 

It’s really just... well, it's exactly like you said, a physical urge. It’s the strangest sensation, almost like there’s a little motor humming away inside me, right in my bones, and it just tells me I need to be moving. Just the other afternoon, for instance, I was sitting with a nice cup of Earl Grey tea, which I usually find so soothing, and I wasn't upset or worried about a single thing in the world. I was actually just looking out the window thinking about how lovely the hydrangeas were blooming this year. But then my feet just started tapping, and my hands felt all restless, and before I knew it, I was up off the sofa and wiping down the baseboards in the hallway with a damp cloth. 

I wasn't worried about the dust, I just... I just physically couldn't stay in that chair another second. It’s like my body has its own agenda, completely separate from my mood, and it just refuses to let me be still. I could be having the best, most peaceful day, and that urge to just get up and do *something* is still there, pushing me along.

I was actually just looking out the window thinking about how lovely the hydrangeas were blooming this year. Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. Actually, my daughter was just saying the other day how lucky I am, because so many of my friends—like Martha, who I mentioned earlier—she has one of those daily pill organizers that honestly looks like a fisherman's tackle box! No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. 

My old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! So no, definitely no new medications for me, and certainly nothing like what you're describing.

I'm constantly fixing things around the house, arranging the cupboards, reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month. And it's not just at home, I keep volunteering for just one more thing down at the church or the library committee. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where, even at 87 years old—when most people my age are perfectly happy resting in a nice armchair—I just have such a terrible time sitting down and being still. I try to sit and watch a program on PBS or read a book, but five minutes in, I’m up again because I remembered a picture frame that needs dusting or a hinge that needs oiling. It's just constant activity, from the moment I wake up until I finally exhaust myself at night, and... well, I just don't know why I can't seem to just stop and relax like a normal person my age. I should be enjoying my retirement, but there's always a project, always something to fuss with, and I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're up doing all these tasks, do you also notice feelings of worry or nervousness running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s not worry at all. My daughter actually asked me that exact same thing—she said, \"Mom, are you anxious about something?\" But I’m really not! My friend Martha, now she’s a worrier. She paces the floor because she’s fretting over her grandson’s grades or whether her pension check is going to be late, and she gets all tied up in knots. But for me, my mind is perfectly fine. I don't have any of those nervous, fearful thoughts about bad things happening. \n\nIt’s really just... well, it's exactly like you said, a physical urge. It’s the strangest sensation, almost like there’s a little motor humming away inside me, right in my bones, and it just tells me I need to be moving. Just the other afternoon, for instance, I was sitting with a nice cup of Earl Grey tea, which I usually find so soothing, and I wasn't upset or worried about a single thing in the world. I was actually just looking out the window thinking about how lovely the hydrangeas were blooming this year. But then my feet just started tapping, and my hands felt all restless, and before I knew it, I was up off the sofa and wiping down the baseboards in the hallway with a damp cloth. \n\nI wasn't worried about the dust, I just... I just physically couldn't stay in that chair another second. It’s like my body has its own agenda, completely separate from my mood, and it just refuses to let me be still. I could be having the best, most peaceful day, and that urge to just get up and do *something* is still there, pushing me along.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently taking any prescription medications, especially anything for nausea, mood, or psychiatric symptoms, that you started around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. Actually, my daughter was just saying the other day how lucky I am, because so many of my friends—like Martha, who I mentioned earlier—she has one of those daily pill organizers that honestly looks like a fisherman's tackle box! She takes something for her stomach, and something else to help her nerves, and goodness knows what else. \n\nBut me? I've always been very fortunate with my health. I really don't take much of anything, and certainly no new prescriptions. I take a daily multivitamin, of course, just the regular kind from the drugstore, and a little calcium with my morning toast because you do have to think about your bones when you get to be my age, but pills for my mood or for nausea or anything psychiatric? No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. \n\nMy old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! Oh, heavens, no. No weight loss at all. Actually, I was just telling my daughter about that. We went down to the department store last Tuesday... or was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s the day the street sweeper comes by and I had to make sure my car was pulled all the way up the driveway. Anyway, I was telling her that I still fit into the exact same tailored navy blue slacks I bought for my eldest granddaughter’s college graduation, and that was a good five or six years ago now. I haven't lost a single pound. I always have half a grapefruit and a piece of dry toast for breakfast every single morning, and I think that routine just keeps me right at the exact same weight I've been since my late sixties. 

And as for feeling warm or sweaty, oh, definitely not. If anything, I'm always the one sneaking over to the hallway to turn the thermostat up a degree or two! You know how it is when you get older, your blood just gets a little thin, I suppose. I always keep a nice knit cardigan draped over the back of my armchair—not that I spend much time actually sitting in that chair to use it, like I told you! But no, I'm never sweaty. My late husband, Arthur, bless his heart, he was the one who was always warm. He’d have the ceiling fan going right through November, but I’m usually perfectly comfortable, maybe even a little chilly if the wind catches that draft under the patio door. 

And my heart certainly doesn't race, no. It just beats away nice and steady, regular as a grandfather clock. Every time the nurse checks my pulse down at the pharmacy when I go in for my seasonal flu shot, she always says it’s just beautiful. So it’s really nothing like that. My heart and my breathing feel completely calm and normal. It’s just... well, it’s just my hands and my feet that seem to think we need to be constantly on the move!

Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that at all. I sleep just fine, thank you very much. I mean, I don't sleep like a teenager anymore, of course—my grandson, he can sleep right through until noon on a Saturday if you let him, which I just don't understand, how can you waste half the day like that? But for me, I usually go to bed right after the ten o'clock news. I like to watch the local broadcast, just to see what the weather is going to do, though honestly they get it wrong half the time anyway. But I go to bed, I read a few pages of my mystery novel—I love those cozy little mysteries set in English villages, they're just so charming—and then I turn out the light and I sleep right through until about six in the morning. 

And when I wake up, I feel rested, just normal rested, you know? Not like I'm bursting with some kind of wild energy or anything. I just get up, put on my slippers—the pink ones with the little rubber soles so I don't slip on the kitchen linoleum—and start my day. I certainly don't feel like I need *less* sleep. If I stay up late for a holiday party or something, I definitely feel it the next day! 

And my thoughts... no, they don't race. They just go at their normal, everyday pace. I think about what I need to get at the grocery store, or whether I need to water the African violets, or what I'm going to wear to church on Sunday. Just regular thoughts, one at a time, nice and orderly. It's not like a jumble of things rushing through my head at all. Like I said before, my mind is perfectly calm and quiet. It's just my body that wants to keep going, going, going. My thoughts are perfectly content to just sit still, even if my feet aren't!

I've been thinking about coming in for a while now, and my daughter actually finally pushed me to make the call. I'm constantly fixing things around the house, arranging the cupboards, reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month. And it's not just at home, I keep volunteering for just one more thing down at the church or the library committee. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where, even at 87 years old—when most people my age are perfectly happy resting in a nice armchair—I just have such a terrible time sitting down and being still. I try to sit and watch a program on PBS or read a book, but five minutes in, I’m up again because I remembered a picture frame that needs dusting or a hinge that needs oiling. It's just constant activity, from the moment I wake up until I finally exhaust myself at night, and... well, I just don't know why I can't seem to just stop and relax like a normal person my age. I should be enjoying my retirement, but there's always a project, always something to fuss with, and I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're up doing all these tasks, do you also notice feelings of worry or nervousness running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s not worry at all. My daughter actually asked me that exact same thing—she said, \"Mom, are you anxious about something?\" But I’m really not! My friend Martha, now she’s a worrier. She paces the floor because she’s fretting over her grandson’s grades or whether her pension check is going to be late, and she gets all tied up in knots. But for me, my mind is perfectly fine. I don't have any of those nervous, fearful thoughts about bad things happening. \n\nIt’s really just... well, it's exactly like you said, a physical urge. It’s the strangest sensation, almost like there’s a little motor humming away inside me, right in my bones, and it just tells me I need to be moving. Just the other afternoon, for instance, I was sitting with a nice cup of Earl Grey tea, which I usually find so soothing, and I wasn't upset or worried about a single thing in the world. I was actually just looking out the window thinking about how lovely the hydrangeas were blooming this year. But then my feet just started tapping, and my hands felt all restless, and before I knew it, I was up off the sofa and wiping down the baseboards in the hallway with a damp cloth. \n\nI wasn't worried about the dust, I just... I just physically couldn't stay in that chair another second. It’s like my body has its own agenda, completely separate from my mood, and it just refuses to let me be still. I could be having the best, most peaceful day, and that urge to just get up and do *something* is still there, pushing me along.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently taking any prescription medications, especially anything for nausea, mood, or psychiatric symptoms, that you started around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. Actually, my daughter was just saying the other day how lucky I am, because so many of my friends—like Martha, who I mentioned earlier—she has one of those daily pill organizers that honestly looks like a fisherman's tackle box! She takes something for her stomach, and something else to help her nerves, and goodness knows what else. \n\nBut me? I've always been very fortunate with my health. I really don't take much of anything, and certainly no new prescriptions. I take a daily multivitamin, of course, just the regular kind from the drugstore, and a little calcium with my morning toast because you do have to think about your bones when you get to be my age, but pills for my mood or for nausea or anything psychiatric? No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. \n\nMy old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! So no, definitely no new medications for me, and certainly nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually warm or sweaty, or a racing heartbeat over this past year?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No weight loss at all. Actually, I was just telling my daughter about that. We went down to the department store last Tuesday... or was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s the day the street sweeper comes by and I had to make sure my car was pulled all the way up the driveway. Anyway, I was telling her that I still fit into the exact same tailored navy blue slacks I bought for my eldest granddaughter’s college graduation, and that was a good five or six years ago now. I haven't lost a single pound. I always have half a grapefruit and a piece of dry toast for breakfast every single morning, and I think that routine just keeps me right at the exact same weight I've been since my late sixties. \n\nAnd as for feeling warm or sweaty, oh, definitely not. If anything, I'm always the one sneaking over to the hallway to turn the thermostat up a degree or two! You know how it is when you get older, your blood just gets a little thin, I suppose. I always keep a nice knit cardigan draped over the back of my armchair—not that I spend much time actually sitting in that chair to use it, like I told you! But no, I'm never sweaty. My late husband, Arthur, bless his heart, he was the one who was always warm. He’d have the ceiling fan going right through November, but I’m usually perfectly comfortable, maybe even a little chilly if the wind catches that draft under the patio door. \n\nAnd my heart certainly doesn't race, no. It just beats away nice and steady, regular as a grandfather clock. Every time the nurse checks my pulse down at the pharmacy when I go in for my seasonal flu shot, she always says it’s just beautiful. So it’s really nothing like that. My heart and my breathing feel completely calm and normal. It’s just... well, it’s just my hands and my feet that seem to think we need to be constantly on the move!\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed needing much less sleep than usual but still feeling full of energy, or having your thoughts race faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that at all. I sleep just fine, thank you very much. I mean, I don't sleep like a teenager anymore, of course—my grandson, he can sleep right through until noon on a Saturday if you let him, which I just don't understand, how can you waste half the day like that? But for me, I usually go to bed right after the ten o'clock news. I like to watch the local broadcast, just to see what the weather is going to do, though honestly they get it wrong half the time anyway. But I go to bed, I read a few pages of my mystery novel—I love those cozy little mysteries set in English villages, they're just so charming—and then I turn out the light and I sleep right through until about six in the morning. \n\nAnd when I wake up, I feel rested, just normal rested, you know? Not like I'm bursting with some kind of wild energy or anything. I just get up, put on my slippers—the pink ones with the little rubber soles so I don't slip on the kitchen linoleum—and start my day. I certainly don't feel like I need *less* sleep. If I stay up late for a holiday party or something, I definitely feel it the next day! \n\nAnd my thoughts... no, they don't race. They just go at their normal, everyday pace. I think about what I need to get at the grocery store, or whether I need to water the African violets, or what I'm going to wear to church on Sunday. Just regular thoughts, one at a time, nice and orderly. It's not like a jumble of things rushing through my head at all. Like I said before, my mind is perfectly calm and quiet. It's just my body that wants to keep going, going, going. My thoughts are perfectly content to just sit still, even if my feet aren't!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

No hyperthyroid or anxiety features support removing those as differentials. G25.81 (akathisia) and R45.1 (restlessness) remain given purely motor, non-cognitive/affective nature of symptoms without clear causative medication, though longstanding idiopathic pattern raises question of other movement disorder.", "is_final": false}

I take a daily multivitamin, of course, just the regular kind from the drugstore, and a little calcium with my morning toast because you do have to think about your bones when you get to be my age, but pills for my mood or for nausea or anything psychiatric? No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. \n\nMy old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! So no, definitely no new medications for me, and certainly nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually warm or sweaty, or a racing heartbeat over this past year?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No weight loss at all. Actually, I was just telling my daughter about that. We went down to the department store last Tuesday... or was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s the day the street sweeper comes by and I had to make sure my car was pulled all the way up the driveway. Anyway, I was telling her that I still fit into the exact same tailored navy blue slacks I bought for my eldest granddaughter’s college graduation, and that was a good five or six years ago now. I haven't lost a single pound. I always have half a grapefruit and a piece of dry toast for breakfast every single morning, and I think that routine just keeps me right at the exact same weight I've been since my late sixties. \n\nAnd as for feeling warm or sweaty, oh, definitely not. If anything, I'm always the one sneaking over to the hallway to turn the thermostat up a degree or two! You know how it is when you get older, your blood just gets a little thin, I suppose. I always keep a nice knit cardigan draped over the back of my armchair—not that I spend much time actually sitting in that chair to use it, like I told you! But no, I'm never sweaty. My late husband, Arthur, bless his heart, he was the one who was always warm. He’d have the ceiling fan going right through November, but I’m usually perfectly comfortable, maybe even a little chilly if the wind catches that draft under the patio door. \n\nAnd my heart certainly doesn't race, no. It just beats away nice and steady, regular as a grandfather clock. Every time the nurse checks my pulse down at the pharmacy when I go in for my seasonal flu shot, she always says it’s just beautiful. So it’s really nothing like that. My heart and my breathing feel completely calm and normal. It’s just... well, it’s just my hands and my feet that seem to think we need to be constantly on the move!\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed needing much less sleep than usual but still feeling full of energy, or having your thoughts race faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that at all. I sleep just fine, thank you very much. I mean, I don't sleep like a teenager anymore, of course—my grandson, he can sleep right through until noon on a Saturday if you let him, which I just don't understand, how can you waste half the day like that? But for me, I usually go to bed right after the ten o'clock news. I like to watch the local broadcast, just to see what the weather is going to do, though honestly they get it wrong half the time anyway. But I go to bed, I read a few pages of my mystery novel—I love those cozy little mysteries set in English villages, they're just so charming—and then I turn out the light and I sleep right through until about six in the morning. \n\nAnd when I wake up, I feel rested, just normal rested, you know? Not like I'm bursting with some kind of wild energy or anything. I just get up, put on my slippers—the pink ones with the little rubber soles so I don't slip on the kitchen linoleum—and start my day. I certainly don't feel like I need *less* sleep. If I stay up late for a holiday party or something, I definitely feel it the next day! \n\nAnd my thoughts... no, they don't race. They just go at their normal, everyday pace. I think about what I need to get at the grocery store, or whether I need to water the African violets, or what I'm going to wear to church on Sunday. Just regular thoughts, one at a time, nice and orderly. It's not like a jumble of things rushing through my head at all. Like I said before, my mind is perfectly calm and quiet. It's just my body that wants to keep going, going, going. My thoughts are perfectly content to just sit still, even if my feet aren't!\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you or your family noticed any shaking, stiffness, or unusual movements in your hands, arms, or face along with this restlessness?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 87 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that. My hands are steady as a rock, thank the Lord. Actually, my daughter was just over this past Sunday for our usual pot roast dinner—I always do a pot roast on Sundays, with the carrots and the little red potatoes, just like my mother used to make, it's a tradition. Anyway, I was pouring the gravy from this heavy ceramic gravy boat that Arthur bought me for our twenty-fifth anniversary, and my daughter, she watched me pour it right over the meat without spilling a single drop on the good lace tablecloth. She even said to me, "Mom, I hope my hands are that steady when I'm your age!" So, no, definitely no shaking in my hands or arms. 

And no stiffness either, not really. I mean, I suppose I have the usual little creaks when it rains, like anybody who's eighty-seven, but my arms move perfectly fine. I can still reach all the way up to the top shelf in the kitchen pantry to get down my special baking tins when I want to make a batch of snickerdoodles for the grandchildren. 

As for my face, no, no unusual movements there either. I don't twitch or make funny faces, unless you count squinting at the television when they put those tiny little letters at the bottom of the screen during the commercials! But no, honestly, it's just this... this feeling that I just need to be up and doing things. It's not a tremor or a stiffness. My body works just fine, it's just that it wants to work all the time! Even when I'm just sitting there trying to listen to my daughter talk about the politics at her garden club, my fingers will just be tapping on the armrest, or I'll be twisting my rings around, or I'll just have to get up and rearrange the throw pillows... but it's not a shake, you see. It's just me not being able to sit still and relax like a normal person. So no, neither me nor my family have noticed anything like shaking or stiffness at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, heavens, no. It’s certainly not just my legs, and it definitely doesn't wait for the sun to go down! It's an all-day affair, I'm afraid. I mean, yes, my feet will tap, but it’s my hands, too, and really just my whole self needing to be up and doing things. 

Like, just yesterday morning at, oh, it must have been nine o'clock because the Today show was just transitioning to their second hour—I don't care for the later hours as much, they get a bit silly with the cooking segments and all that—I was sitting in my armchair trying to read the morning paper. And I just couldn't sit there. I had to get up and reorganize the spice rack, which I had just organized last month! I took every single little jar out, wiped down the shelf, and put them all back in alphabetical order. 

And as for the evening... well, it doesn't get worse then, no. It's just exactly the same as it is at ten in the morning! When I sit down in the evening to watch Jeopardy—I never miss Jeopardy, you know, Alex Trebek was just wonderful, God rest his soul, though that Ken Jennings is doing a very nice job filling his shoes—I find myself popping up during every single commercial break. I’ll go wipe down the kitchen counters again, or I’ll go check the front porch to make sure the porch light is on, or I'll just pace around the living room fluffing the pillows. 

But it’s not a strange feeling in my legs specifically, you see. It's not an ache or a tingle or anything like that. It’s just this... this motor inside me that runs from the minute I wake up until I finally put myself to bed. I'm just as fidgety at breakfast as I am after dinner. So, no, not just the legs, and certainly not just at night!

I've been thinking about coming in for a while now, and my daughter actually finally pushed me to make the call. You see, for the past year or so, I just feel like I’m always... well, always puttering around. I'm constantly fixing things around the house, arranging the cupboards, reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month. And it's not just at home, I keep volunteering for just one more thing down at the church or the library committee. \n\nIt’s gotten to the point where, even at 87 years old—when most people my age are perfectly happy resting in a nice armchair—I just have such a terrible time sitting down and being still. I try to sit and watch a program on PBS or read a book, but five minutes in, I’m up again because I remembered a picture frame that needs dusting or a hinge that needs oiling. It's just constant activity, from the moment I wake up until I finally exhaust myself at night, and... well, I just don't know why I can't seem to just stop and relax like a normal person my age. I should be enjoying my retirement, but there's always a project, always something to fuss with, and I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're up doing all these tasks, do you also notice feelings of worry or nervousness running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I like to watch the local broadcast, just to see what the weather is going to do, though honestly they get it wrong half the time anyway. But I go to bed, I read a few pages of my mystery novel—I love those cozy little mysteries set in English villages, they're just so charming—and then I turn out the light and I sleep right through until about six in the morning. \n\nAnd when I wake up, I feel rested, just normal rested, you know? Not like I'm bursting with some kind of wild energy or anything. I just get up, put on my slippers—the pink ones with the little rubber soles so I don't slip on the kitchen linoleum—and start my day. I certainly don't feel like I need *less* sleep. If I stay up late for a holiday party or something, I definitely feel it the next day! \n\nAnd my thoughts... no, they don't race. They just go at their normal, everyday pace. I think about what I need to get at the grocery store, or whether I need to water the African violets, or what I'm going to wear to church on Sunday. Just regular thoughts, one at a time, nice and orderly. It's not like a jumble of things rushing through my head at all. Like I said before, my mind is perfectly calm and quiet. It's just my body that wants to keep going, going, going. My thoughts are perfectly content to just sit still, even if my feet aren't!\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any shaking, stiffness, or unusual movements in your hands, arms, or face along with this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that. My hands are steady as a rock, thank the Lord. Actually, my daughter was just over this past Sunday for our usual pot roast dinner—I always do a pot roast on Sundays, with the carrots and the little red potatoes, just like my mother used to make, it's a tradition. Anyway, I was pouring the gravy from this heavy ceramic gravy boat that Arthur bought me for our twenty-fifth anniversary, and my daughter, she watched me pour it right over the meat without spilling a single drop on the good lace tablecloth. She even said to me, \"Mom, I hope my hands are that steady when I'm your age!\" So, no, definitely no shaking in my hands or arms. \n\nAnd no stiffness either, not really. I mean, I suppose I have the usual little creaks when it rains, like anybody who's eighty-seven, but my arms move perfectly fine. I can still reach all the way up to the top shelf in the kitchen pantry to get down my special baking tins when I want to make a batch of snickerdoodles for the grandchildren. \n\nAs for my face, no, no unusual movements there either. I don't twitch or make funny faces, unless you count squinting at the television when they put those tiny little letters at the bottom of the screen during the commercials! But no, honestly, it's just this... this feeling that I just need to be up and doing things. It's not a tremor or a stiffness. My body works just fine, it's just that it wants to work all the time! Even when I'm just sitting there trying to listen to my daughter talk about the politics at her garden club, my fingers will just be tapping on the armrest, or I'll be twisting my rings around, or I'll just have to get up and rearrange the throw pillows... but it's not a shake, you see. It's just me not being able to sit still and relax like a normal person. So no, neither me nor my family have noticed anything like shaking or stiffness at all.\n\nDoctor: Does this urge to move seem to happen mostly in your legs specifically, and have you noticed it gets worse in the evening or at night compared to earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. It’s certainly not just my legs, and it definitely doesn't wait for the sun to go down! It's an all-day affair, I'm afraid. I mean, yes, my feet will tap, but it’s my hands, too, and really just my whole self needing to be up and doing things. \n\nLike, just yesterday morning at, oh, it must have been nine o'clock because the Today show was just transitioning to their second hour—I don't care for the later hours as much, they get a bit silly with the cooking segments and all that—I was sitting in my armchair trying to read the morning paper. And I just couldn't sit there. I had to get up and reorganize the spice rack, which I had just organized last month! I took every single little jar out, wiped down the shelf, and put them all back in alphabetical order. \n\nAnd as for the evening... well, it doesn't get worse then, no. It's just exactly the same as it is at ten in the morning! When I sit down in the evening to watch Jeopardy—I never miss Jeopardy, you know, Alex Trebek was just wonderful, God rest his soul, though that Ken Jennings is doing a very nice job filling his shoes—I find myself popping up during every single commercial break. I’ll go wipe down the kitchen counters again, or I’ll go check the front porch to make sure the porch light is on, or I'll just pace around the living room fluffing the pillows. \n\nBut it’s not a strange feeling in my legs specifically, you see. It's not an ache or a tingle or anything like that. It’s just this... this motor inside me that runs from the minute I wake up until I finally put myself to bed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, back in my forties and fifties, I was certainly a busy woman. I mean, raising children, keeping a home—Arthur was working such long hours back then, so I ran the entire household. I was always doing something, sure, but it wasn't like this. I could still sit down in the evening and knit a whole sweater, or watch a movie without feeling like I was going to jump right out of my skin! I could relax. 

No, this... this absolute constant need to just keep moving, it's really been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now. I know exactly how long it's been because that's when everything happened with the house. 

We had that terrible storm—oh, the wind was just howling, it took down the big beautiful oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street, just completely uprooted it!—and it did a real number on my roof. Well, the home repairs were just a nightmare to organize. The contractors never call you back when they say they will, do they? They tell you they'll be there between eight and noon, and you sit there waiting, and they show up at three in the afternoon! And the insurance bills... oh, heavens, the paperwork was endless. They fought me on every little penny, sending me form after form. It was so stressful, all those phone calls and people tracking mud on my good carpets. 

So, just to get out of the house and away from the noise and the dust, those insurance bills and home repairs pushed me into taking on more at the senior center. I thought, well, if my house is going to be a construction zone, I might as well make myself useful somewhere else, right? So I started organizing the Tuesday morning bake sales, and then they needed help with the charity drives, so I took over the scheduling for that, and before I knew it, I was running myself absolutely ragged over there. 

But that's when this really started to get so noticeable. Ever since I took on all that extra work at the center a year and five months ago, I just haven't been able to turn that motor off. Even now that the roof is fixed—they used those architectural shingles, very nice, guaranteed for thirty years, though at eighty-seven I certainly won't be needing them for that long!—but yes, even with the house completely put back together and the insurance finally settled, I just can't seem to stop moving. It's like I got wound up tight as a top back then and nobody knows how to unwind me! So no, it hasn't always been this way, not at all.

I'm constantly fixing things around the house, arranging the cupboards, reorganizing the linen closet for the third time this month. I try to sit and watch a program on PBS or read a book, but five minutes in, I’m up again because I remembered a picture frame that needs dusting or a hinge that needs oiling. It's just constant activity, from the moment I wake up until I finally exhaust myself at night, and... well, I just don't know why I can't seem to just stop and relax like a normal person my age. I should be enjoying my retirement, but there's always a project, always something to fuss with, and I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're up doing all these tasks, do you also notice feelings of worry or nervousness running through your mind, or is it mainly just a physical urge to keep moving?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. It’s not worry at all. My daughter actually asked me that exact same thing—she said, \"Mom, are you anxious about something?\" But I’m really not! My friend Martha, now she’s a worrier. She paces the floor because she’s fretting over her grandson’s grades or whether her pension check is going to be late, and she gets all tied up in knots. But for me, my mind is perfectly fine. I don't have any of those nervous, fearful thoughts about bad things happening. \n\nIt’s really just... well, it's exactly like you said, a physical urge. It’s the strangest sensation, almost like there’s a little motor humming away inside me, right in my bones, and it just tells me I need to be moving. Just the other afternoon, for instance, I was sitting with a nice cup of Earl Grey tea, which I usually find so soothing, and I wasn't upset or worried about a single thing in the world. I was actually just looking out the window thinking about how lovely the hydrangeas were blooming this year. But then my feet just started tapping, and my hands felt all restless, and before I knew it, I was up off the sofa and wiping down the baseboards in the hallway with a damp cloth. \n\nI wasn't worried about the dust, I just... I just physically couldn't stay in that chair another second. It’s like my body has its own agenda, completely separate from my mood, and it just refuses to let me be still. I could be having the best, most peaceful day, and that urge to just get up and do *something* is still there, pushing me along.\n\nDoctor: Are you currently taking any prescription medications, especially anything for nausea, mood, or psychiatric symptoms, that you started around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. Actually, my daughter was just saying the other day how lucky I am, because so many of my friends—like Martha, who I mentioned earlier—she has one of those daily pill organizers that honestly looks like a fisherman's tackle box! She takes something for her stomach, and something else to help her nerves, and goodness knows what else. \n\nBut me? I've always been very fortunate with my health. I really don't take much of anything, and certainly no new prescriptions. I take a daily multivitamin, of course, just the regular kind from the drugstore, and a little calcium with my morning toast because you do have to think about your bones when you get to be my age, but pills for my mood or for nausea or anything psychiatric? No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. \n\nMy old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! So no, definitely no new medications for me, and certainly nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually warm or sweaty, or a racing heartbeat over this past year?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No weight loss at all. Actually, I was just telling my daughter about that. We went down to the department store last Tuesday... or was it Wednesday? No, it was Tuesday, because that’s the day the street sweeper comes by and I had to make sure my car was pulled all the way up the driveway. Anyway, I was telling her that I still fit into the exact same tailored navy blue slacks I bought for my eldest granddaughter’s college graduation, and that was a good five or six years ago now. I haven't lost a single pound. I always have half a grapefruit and a piece of dry toast for breakfast every single morning, and I think that routine just keeps me right at the exact same weight I've been since my late sixties. \n\nAnd as for feeling warm or sweaty, oh, definitely not. If anything, I'm always the one sneaking over to the hallway to turn the thermostat up a degree or two! You know how it is when you get older, your blood just gets a little thin, I suppose. I always keep a nice knit cardigan draped over the back of my armchair—not that I spend much time actually sitting in that chair to use it, like I told you! But no, I'm never sweaty. My late husband, Arthur, bless his heart, he was the one who was always warm. He’d have the ceiling fan going right through November, but I’m usually perfectly comfortable, maybe even a little chilly if the wind catches that draft under the patio door. \n\nAnd my heart certainly doesn't race, no. It just beats away nice and steady, regular as a grandfather clock. Every time the nurse checks my pulse down at the pharmacy when I go in for my seasonal flu shot, she always says it’s just beautiful. So it’s really nothing like that. My heart and my breathing feel completely calm and normal. It’s just... well, it’s just my hands and my feet that seem to think we need to be constantly on the move!\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed needing much less sleep than usual but still feeling full of energy, or having your thoughts race faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. Nothing like that at all. I sleep just fine, thank you very much. I mean, I don't sleep like a teenager anymore, of course—my grandson, he can sleep right through until noon on a Saturday if you let him, which I just don't understand, how can you waste half the day like that? But for me, I usually go to bed right after the ten o'clock news. I like to watch the local broadcast, just to see what the weather is going to do, though honestly they get it wrong half the time anyway. But I go to bed, I read a few pages of my mystery novel—I love those cozy little mysteries set in English villages, they're just so charming—and then I turn out the light and I sleep right through until about six in the morning. \n\nAnd when I wake up, I feel rested, just normal rested, you know? Not like I'm bursting with some kind of wild energy or anything. I just get up, put on my slippers—the pink ones with the little rubber soles so I don't slip on the kitchen linoleum—and start my day. I certainly don't feel like I need *less* sleep. If I stay up late for a holiday party or something, I definitely feel it the next day! \n\nAnd my thoughts... no, they don't race. They just go at their normal, everyday pace. I think about what I need to get at the grocery store, or whether I need to water the African violets, or what I'm going to wear to church on Sunday. Just regular thoughts, one at a time, nice and orderly. It's not like a jumble of things rushing through my head at all. Like I said before, my mind is perfectly calm and quiet. It's just my body that wants to keep going, going, going. My thoughts are perfectly content to just sit still, even if my feet aren't!\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any shaking, stiffness, or unusual movements in your hands, arms, or face along with this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that. My hands are steady as a rock, thank the Lord. Actually, my daughter was just over this past Sunday for our usual pot roast dinner—I always do a pot roast on Sundays, with the carrots and the little red potatoes, just like my mother used to make, it's a tradition. Anyway, I was pouring the gravy from this heavy ceramic gravy boat that Arthur bought me for our twenty-fifth anniversary, and my daughter, she watched me pour it right over the meat without spilling a single drop on the good lace tablecloth. She even said to me, \"Mom, I hope my hands are that steady when I'm your age!\" So, no, definitely no shaking in my hands or arms. \n\nAnd no stiffness either, not really. I mean, I suppose I have the usual little creaks when it rains, like anybody who's eighty-seven, but my arms move perfectly fine. I can still reach all the way up to the top shelf in the kitchen pantry to get down my special baking tins when I want to make a batch of snickerdoodles for the grandchildren. \n\nAs for my face, no, no unusual movements there either. I don't twitch or make funny faces, unless you count squinting at the television when they put those tiny little letters at the bottom of the screen during the commercials! But no, honestly, it's just this... this feeling that I just need to be up and doing things. It's not a tremor or a stiffness. My body works just fine, it's just that it wants to work all the time! Even when I'm just sitting there trying to listen to my daughter talk about the politics at her garden club, my fingers will just be tapping on the armrest, or I'll be twisting my rings around, or I'll just have to get up and rearrange the throw pillows... but it's not a shake, you see. It's just me not being able to sit still and relax like a normal person. So no, neither me nor my family have noticed anything like shaking or stiffness at all.\n\nDoctor: Does this urge to move seem to happen mostly in your legs specifically, and have you noticed it gets worse in the evening or at night compared to earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. It’s certainly not just my legs, and it definitely doesn't wait for the sun to go down! It's an all-day affair, I'm afraid. I mean, yes, my feet will tap, but it’s my hands, too, and really just my whole self needing to be up and doing things. \n\nLike, just yesterday morning at, oh, it must have been nine o'clock because the Today show was just transitioning to their second hour—I don't care for the later hours as much, they get a bit silly with the cooking segments and all that—I was sitting in my armchair trying to read the morning paper. And I just couldn't sit there. I had to get up and reorganize the spice rack, which I had just organized last month! I took every single little jar out, wiped down the shelf, and put them all back in alphabetical order. \n\nAnd as for the evening... well, it doesn't get worse then, no. It's just exactly the same as it is at ten in the morning! When I sit down in the evening to watch Jeopardy—I never miss Jeopardy, you know, Alex Trebek was just wonderful, God rest his soul, though that Ken Jennings is doing a very nice job filling his shoes—I find myself popping up during every single commercial break. I’ll go wipe down the kitchen counters again, or I’ll go check the front porch to make sure the porch light is on, or I'll just pace around the living room fluffing the pillows. \n\nBut it’s not a strange feeling in my legs specifically, you see. It's not an ache or a tingle or anything like that. It’s just this... this motor inside me that runs from the minute I wake up until I finally put myself to bed. I'm just as fidgety at breakfast as I am after dinner. So, no, not just the legs, and certainly not just at night!\n\nDoctor: Thinking back over your life—say, in your 40s or 50s—would you describe yourself as having always been a very busy, active person who liked to stay in motion, or is this constant need to be doing something new for you over this past year?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, back in my forties and fifties, I was certainly a busy woman. I mean, raising children, keeping a home—Arthur was working such long hours back then, so I ran the entire household. I was always doing something, sure, but it wasn't like this. I could still sit down in the evening and knit a whole sweater, or watch a movie without feeling like I was going to jump right out of my skin! I could relax. \n\nNo, this... this absolute constant need to just keep moving, it's really been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now. I know exactly how long it's been because that's when everything happened with the house. \n\nWe had that terrible storm—oh, the wind was just howling, it took down the big beautiful oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street, just completely uprooted it!—and it did a real number on my roof. Well, the home repairs were just a nightmare to organize. The contractors never call you back when they say they will, do they? They tell you they'll be there between eight and noon, and you sit there waiting, and they show up at three in the afternoon! And the insurance bills... oh, heavens, the paperwork was endless. They fought me on every little penny, sending me form after form. It was so stressful, all those phone calls and people tracking mud on my good carpets. \n\nSo, just to get out of the house and away from the noise and the dust, those insurance bills and home repairs pushed me into taking on more at the senior center. I thought, well, if my house is going to be a construction zone, I might as well make myself useful somewhere else, right? So I started organizing the Tuesday morning bake sales, and then they needed help with the charity drives, so I took over the scheduling for that, and before I knew it, I was running myself absolutely ragged over there. \n\nBut that's when this really started to get so noticeable. Ever since I took on all that extra work at the center a year and five months ago, I just haven't been able to turn that motor off. Even now that the roof is fixed—they used those architectural shingles, very nice, guaranteed for thirty years, though at eighty-seven I certainly won't be needing them for that long!—but yes, even with the house completely put back together and the insurance finally settled, I just can't seem to stop moving. It's like I got wound up tight as a top back then and nobody knows how to unwind me! So no, it hasn't always been this way, not at all."
  }

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nOh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. Actually, my daughter was just saying the other day how lucky I am, because so many of my friends—like Martha, who I mentioned earlier—she has one of those daily pill organizers that honestly looks like a fisherman's tackle box! She takes something for her stomach, and something else to help her nerves, and goodness knows what else. \n\nBut me? I've always been very fortunate with my health. I really don't take much of anything, and certainly no new prescriptions. I take a daily multivitamin, of course, just the regular kind from the drugstore, and a little calcium with my morning toast because you do have to think about your bones when you get to be my age, but pills for my mood or for nausea or anything psychiatric? No, never. I haven't started any new medications in... well, I can't even remember how long it's been. \n\nMy old primary care doctor, Dr. Henderson—he retired a few years back, such a lovely man, always wore those little polka-dot bow ties—he used to say I had the constitution of a horse. He always told me to just keep doing whatever I was doing because it was working. So no, there hasn't been a single change in my medicine cabinet. I've never liked the idea of taking pills unless it's an absolute emergency anyway. It always seems like you take one thing, and it upsets your stomach, and then they give you another pill for the stomach, and before you know it, you're rattling when you walk! So no, definitely no new medications for me, and certainly nothing like what you're describing.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. I haven't lost a single pound. I always have half a grapefruit and a piece of dry toast for breakfast every single morning, and I think that routine just keeps me right at the exact same weight I've been since my late sixties. \n\nAnd as for feeling warm or sweaty, oh, definitely not. If anything, I'm always the one sneaking over to the hallway to turn the thermostat up a degree or two! You know how it is when you get older, your blood just gets a little thin, I suppose. I always keep a nice knit cardigan draped over the back of my armchair—not that I spend much time actually sitting in that chair to use it, like I told you! But no, I'm never sweaty. My late husband, Arthur, bless his heart, he was the one who was always warm. He’d have the ceiling fan going right through November, but I’m usually perfectly comfortable, maybe even a little chilly if the wind catches that draft under the patio door. \n\nAnd my heart certainly doesn't race, no. It just beats away nice and steady, regular as a grandfather clock. Every time the nurse checks my pulse down at the pharmacy when I go in for my seasonal flu shot, she always says it’s just beautiful. So it’s really nothing like that. My heart and my breathing feel completely calm and normal.
